\displaystyle\mathsf{id}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})},(\mathrm{T},c)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

Recall that the
2
-category
\mathsf{CAT}
of categories admits inserters and equifiers, and notice that
(\mathrm{T},c)
is a strong tangent morphism which preserves the fundamental tangent limits. Thus, by Proposition
4.4
, the inserter
(\mathbb{I},\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{I}})\colon\mathsf{id}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})}\Rightarrow(\mathrm{T},c)
of
\mathsf{id}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})}
and
(\mathrm{T},c)
is well-defined in
\mathsf{TNGCAT}
. Concretely,
\mathbb{I}
is the category of pairs
(M,v)
, formed by an object
M
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and a morphism
v\colon M=\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}(M)\to\mathrm{T}M
. A morphism
f\colon(M,v)\to(N,u)
is a morphism
f\colon M\to N
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
which commutes with
v
and
u
, namely,
u\circ f=\mathrm{T}f\circ v
. By unwrapping the proof of Proposition
4.4
, one can also show that the tangent bundle functor
\mathrm{T}^{\mathbb{I}}
sends each
(M,v)
to
(\mathrm{T}M,v_{\mathrm{T}})
, where

\displaystyle v_{\mathrm{T}}\colon\mathrm{T}M\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}v}\mathrm{T}^{2}M\xrightarrow{c}\mathrm{T}^{2}M

and each morphism
f
to
\mathrm{T}f
. Finally, the structural natural transformations of
\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{I}}
are defined as in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Notice that
(\mathbb{I},\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{I}})
resembles the tangent category of vector fields of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
with only one difference: the morphisms
v\colon M\to\mathrm{T}M
need not satisfy any constraint. To impose each
v
to be a section of the projection, namely, to satisfy the equation
p\circ v=\mathsf{id}_{M}
, we need to use an equifier. Consider the two parallel tangent morphisms

\displaystyle V,(\mathrm{T},c)\circ V\colon(\mathbb{I},\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{I}})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

where
V\colon(\mathbb{I},\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{I}})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is the universal strict tangent morphism of the inserter. Consider also the two tangent
2
-morphisms

\displaystyle V\xrightarrow{\hat{\theta}}V\circ(\mathrm{T},c)\xrightarrow{Vp}V

\displaystyle\mathsf{id}_{V}\colon V\to V

where
\hat{\theta}\colon V\circ\mathsf{id}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})}\Rightarrow V\circ(\mathrm{T},c)
is the universal
2
-morphism of the inserter
(\mathbb{I},\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{I}})
. Let
(\mathbb{E},\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{E}})
be the equifier of
Vp\circ\hat{\theta}
and
\mathsf{id}_{V}
in
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
. Concretely,
(\mathbb{E},\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{E}})
is the tangent subcategory of
(\mathbb{I},\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{I}})
spanned by those objects
(M,v)
of
(\mathbb{I},\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{I}})
satisfying

\displaystyle(Vp\circ\hat{\theta})_{(M,v)}=\mathsf{id}_{(M,v)}

namely:

\displaystyle(M\xrightarrow{v}\mathrm{T}M\xrightarrow{p_{M}}M)=(M\xrightarrow{\mathsf{id}_{M}}M)

Thus,
(\mathbb{E},\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{E}})
is exactly the tangent category of vector fields of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Theorem 5.23
.

Consider a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
in a
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
and suppose that the inserter
\mathbb{I}\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}
and the equifier
\mathbb{E}\colon Vp\circ\hat{\theta}\Rightarrow\mathsf{id}_{V}
exist in
{\mathbf{K}}
. Thus,
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
admits the construction of vector fields in
{\mathbf{K}}
and the tangentad
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of vector fields of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is the equifier
(\mathbb{E},\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{E}})
.

Proof.

Thanks to Propositions
4.4
and
4.6
,
(\mathbb{I},\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{I}})
and
(\mathbb{E},\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{E}})
are an inserter and an equifier in
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
, respectively. Consider a tangentad
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
and let us unwrap the definition of the category
\mathsf{EQUIF}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime};Vp\circ\hat{\theta},\mathsf{id}_{V}]
. An object of this category is a lax tangent morphism
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{I},\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{I}})
satisfying the following equation:

\displaystyle(Vp\circ\hat{\theta})_{F}=\mathsf{id}_{V\circ F}

A morphism of
\mathsf{EQUIF}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime};Vp\circ\hat{\theta},\mathsf{id}_{V}]
is a tangent
2
-morphism
\varphi\colon(F,\alpha)\Rightarrow(G,\beta)
. By postcomposing an object
(F,\alpha)
by
V\colon(\mathbb{I},\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{I}})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
we obtain a lax tangent morphism
V\circ(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
equipped with a
2
-morphism:

\displaystyle v\colon V\circ(F,\alpha)\xrightarrow{\hat{\theta}_{(F,\alpha)}}(\mathrm{T},c)\circ V\circ(F,\alpha)=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(V\circ(F,\alpha))

Moreover, since
(Vp\circ\hat{\theta})_{F}=\mathsf{id}_{V\circ F}
, the
2
-morphism
v\colon V\circ(F,\alpha)\to\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(V\circ(F,\alpha))
defines a vector field in the Hom-tangent category
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
. This construction extends to a functor

\displaystyle\mathsf{EQUIF}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime};Vp\circ\hat{\theta},\mathsf{id}_{V}]\to\mathsf{VF}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]

Conversely, given a vector field
v\colon(H,\gamma)\to\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(H,\gamma)
in the Hom-tangent category
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
, by virtue of the universal property of the inserter, there exists a tangent morphism
(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muH\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\gamma\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{I},\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{I}})
such that
V\circ(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muH\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\gamma\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)=(H,\gamma)
and
\hat{\theta}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muH\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}=v
. Furthermore, since
p_{H}\circ v=\mathsf{id}_{(H,\gamma)}
:

\displaystyle(Vp\circ\hat{\theta})_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muH\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}=Vp_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muH\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\circ\hat{\theta}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muH\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}=p_{V\circ\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muH\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\circ v=p_{H}\circ v=\mathsf{id}_{H}=(\mathsf{id}_{V})_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muH\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}

Therefore, by the universal property of the inserter,
(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muH\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\gamma\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)
is an object of
\mathsf{EQUIF}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime};Vp\circ\hat{\theta},\mathsf{id}_{V}]
. One can show that this defines an isomorphism:

\displaystyle\mathsf{EQUIF}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime};Vp\circ\hat{\theta},\mathsf{id}_{V}]\cong\mathsf{VF}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]

However, by the universal property of the equifier,
\mathsf{EQUIF}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime};Vp\circ\hat{\theta},\mathsf{id}_{V}]
is also isomorphic to the Hom-tangent category
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{E},\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{E}}]
. Thus, the equifier
(\mathbb{E},\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{E}})
is the tangentad of vector fields of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.
∎

When a
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
admits inserters and equifiers, by Proposition
5.23
, we can construct vector fields for any tangentads.

Corollary 5.24
.

A
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
which admits inserters and equifiers admits the construction of vector fields.

5.5
Applications

In Section
2.5
, we listed some examples of tangentads. In this section, we compute the construction of vector fields for each of these examples.

Tangent monads

Consider a tangent monad
(S,\alpha)
on a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Since
(S,\alpha)
is a lax tangent morphism, by Proposition
5.10
,
(S,\alpha)
sends each vector field
(M,v)
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
to a vector field
(SM,v_{S})
, where
v_{S}
is defined by:

\displaystyle SM\xrightarrow{Sv}S\mathrm{T}M\xrightarrow{\alpha}\mathrm{T}SM

Furthermore, if
f\colon(M,v)\to(M^{\prime},v^{\prime})
is a morphism of vector fields, by naturality of
\alpha
, so is
Sf\colon(SM,v_{S})\to(SM^{\prime},v^{\prime}_{S})
. Since the unit
\eta
and the multiplication
\mu
of the monad
S
are compatible with
\alpha
, the monad
S
lifts to a monad
\mathsf{VF}(S,\alpha)
on
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
which is compatible with the tangent structure. Thus,
(\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}),\mathsf{VF}(S,\alpha))
defines a tangent monad.

Theorem 5.25
.

The
2
-category
\mathsf{MND}
of monads admits the construction of vector fields. In particular, the tangentad of vector fields of a tangent monad
(S,\alpha)
on a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is the tangent monad
\mathsf{VF}(S,\alpha)
.

Proof.

Even though the
2
-category
\mathsf{MND}
does not admit all inserters and equifiers, it admits inserters
\mathbb{I}\colon F\Rightarrow G
and equifiers
\mathbb{E}\colon\varphi\to\psi\colon F\Rightarrow G
whose base
1
-morphism
F
is a strong morphism of monads, namely, whose distributive law with the monad is invertible (in
[
17
, Propositions 4.3 and 4.3]
it was proven when
F
is strict, but the proof extends readily for strong morphisms). Luckily, the inserters and equifiers required to construct the tangentads of vector fields fit into this class. Thus, by Theorem
5.23
,
\mathsf{MND}
admits the construction of vector fields. By unwrapping the definitions of inserters and equifiers in
\mathsf{MND}
, one finds out that the tangentad of vector fields of a tangent monad
(S,\alpha)
is precisely
\mathsf{VF}(S,\alpha)
.
∎

Tangent fibrations

In this section, we compute the construction of vector fields for tangent fibrations.

Theorem 5.26
.

The
2
-category
\mathsf{FIB}
of fibrations admits the construction of vector fields. In particular, the tangentad of vector fields of a tangent fibration
\Pi\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is the tangent fibration
\mathsf{VF}(\Pi)\colon\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
which sends a vector field
(E,w)
of
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
to
(\Pi(E),\Pi(w))
.

Proof.

The
2
-category
\mathsf{FIB}
is the
2
-category of pseudo-algebras of a
2
-monad
[
24
]
. Moreover, the
2
-category of pseudo-algebras of a
2
-monad lifts PIE limits
[
4
, Section 2]
. Thus,
\mathsf{FIB}
admits inserters and equifiers. Therefore, by Theorem
5.23
,
\mathsf{FIB}
admits the construction of vector fields. By unwrapping this construction, one finds out that the tangentad of vector fields of a tangent fibration
\Pi
is precisely
\mathsf{VF}(\Pi)
.
∎

Tangent indexed categories

[
19
, Theorem 5.5]
proves that the
2
-categories
\mathsf{TNGFIB}
and
\mathsf{TNGINDX}
of tangent fibrations and tangent indexed categories are equivalent via a Grothendieck-like construction. In this section, we utilize this equivalence to construct the vector fields of tangent indexed categories. First, let us prove that equivalences of tangentads induce equivalences of the tangentads of vector fields.

Proposition 5.27
.

Let
{\mathbf{K}}
and
{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}
be two
2
-categories and suppose there is a
2
-equivalence
\Xi\colon\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})\simeq\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}}^{\prime})
of the
2
-categories of tangentads of
{\mathbf{K}}
and
{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}
. If a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
{\mathbf{K}}
admits the construction of vector fields and
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
denotes the tangentad of vector fields of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, so does
\Xi(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and the tangentad of vector fields of
\Xi(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is
\Xi(\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))
.

Proof.

First, notice that a
2
-equivalence
\Xi\colon\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})\simeq\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}}^{\prime})
lifts to a
2
-equivalence

\displaystyle\mathsf{VF}(\Xi)\colon\mathsf{VF}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]\to\mathsf{VF}[\Xi(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\>|\>\Xi(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})]

between the tangent category of vector fields in each Hom-tangent category
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
of
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
to the one of vector fields in the corresponding Hom-tangent category
[\Xi(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\>|\>\Xi(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})]
of
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}}^{\prime})
. Furthermore, the functor

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathsf{VF}(\Xi)(\mathcal{V})}\colon[\Xi(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\>|\>\Xi(\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))]\to\mathsf{VF}[\Xi(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\>|\>\Xi(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})]

coincides with the functor
\Xi(\Gamma_{\mathcal{V}})
, thus, in particular,
\Gamma_{\mathsf{VF}(\Xi)(\mathcal{V})}
becomes an isomorphism of tangent categories. Thus,
\Xi(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
admits the construction of vector fields and
\Xi(\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))
is the tangentad of vector fields of
\Xi(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.
∎

Proposition
5.27
together with the Grothendieck
2
-equivalence
\mathsf{TNGFIB}\cong\mathsf{TNGINDX}
allows us to compute the vector fields of tangent indexed categories. Consider a tangent indexed category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};\mathscr{I},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
whose underlying indexed category
\mathscr{I}\colon\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
sends an object
M
of
\mathbb{X}
to the category
\mathbb{X}^{M}
and a morphism
f\colon M\to N
to the functor
f^{\ast}\colon\mathbb{X}^{N}\to\mathbb{X}^{M}
. Define
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};\mathscr{I},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
as follows:

:

\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
;

Indexed tangent bundle functor

The indexed tangent bundle functor
\mathsf{VF}(\mathrm{T}^{\prime})
consists of the list of functors

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}^{\prime(M,v)}\colon\mathsf{VF}^{(M,v)}\to\mathsf{VF}^{(\mathrm{T}M,v_{\mathrm{T}})}

which send a pair
(E,w)\in\mathsf{VF}^{(M,v)}

w_{\mathrm{T}^{\prime M}})

\displaystyle w_{\mathrm{T}^{\prime M}}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\prime M}E\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}^{\prime M}w}\mathrm{T}^{\prime M}v^{\ast}\mathrm{T}^{\prime M}E\xrightarrow{\xi^{v}}(\mathrm{T}v)^{\ast}\mathrm{T}^{\prime\mathrm{T}M}\mathrm{T}^{\prime M}E\xrightarrow{(\mathrm{T}v)^{\ast}c^{\prime M}}(\mathrm{T}v)^{\ast}c^{\ast}\mathrm{T}^{\prime\mathrm{T}M}\mathrm{T}^{\prime M}E\xrightarrow{\mathscr{I}_{2}}(v_{\mathrm{T}})^{\ast}\mathrm{T}^{\prime\mathrm{T}M}\mathrm{T}^{\prime M}E

Moreover,
\mathrm{T}^{\prime(M,v)}

\mathrm{T}^{\prime M}\varphi
. The distributors of

\xi^{f}

\mathrm{T}^{\prime M}
;

\mathsf{VF}(\mathscr{I})
are the same as for
\mathscr{I}
.

Theorem 5.28
.

The
2
-category
\mathsf{INDX}
of indexed categories admits the construction of vector fields. In particular, given a tangent indexed category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};\mathscr{I},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
, the tangent indexed category of vector fields of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};\mathscr{I},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
is the tangent indexed category
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};\mathscr{I},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})=(\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}),\mathsf{VF}(\mathscr{I}),\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{T}^{\prime}))
.

Proof.

Thanks to Proposition
5.27
, the Grothendieck
2
-equivalence
\mathsf{FIB}\simeq\mathsf{INDX}
of
[
19
, Theorem 5.5]
implies that
\mathsf{INDX}
admits the construction of vector fields. Furthermore,
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};\mathscr{I},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
corresponds via the Grothendieck equivalence to
\mathsf{VF}(\Pi_{(\mathscr{I},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})})
, where
\Pi_{(\mathscr{I},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})}
is the tangent fibration associated to
(\mathscr{I},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
. By spelling out the details, one finds out that this corresponds to the tangent indexed category
(\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}),\mathsf{VF}(\mathscr{I}),\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{T}^{\prime}))
.
∎

Tangent split restriction categories

In this section, we compute the construction of vector fields for tangent split restriction categories.

Consider a tangent split restriction category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and let
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
denote the category of pairs
(M,v)
formed by an object
M
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and a total map
v\colon M\to\mathrm{T}M
, namely,
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muv\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mathsf{id}_{M}
, and whose morphisms
f\colon(M,v)\to(N,u)
are morphisms
f\colon M\to N
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
which strictly commute with
v
and
u
, namely,
u\circ f=\mathrm{T}f\circ v
and whose restriction idempotent
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muf\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\colon M\to M
commutes with
v
, namely,
v\circ\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muf\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mathrm{T}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muf\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\circ v
. The tangent split restriction structure on
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
lifts to
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. In particular, the tangent bundle functor sends each pair
(M,v)
to
(\mathrm{T}M,v_{\mathrm{T}})
, where:

\displaystyle v_{\mathrm{T}}\colon\mathrm{T}M\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}v}\mathrm{T}^{2}M\xrightarrow{c}\mathrm{T}^{2}M

Theorem 5.29
.

The
2
-category
\mathsf{SRSTR}
of split restriction categories admits the construction of vector fields. In particular, the tangentad of vector fields of a tangent split restriction category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is the tangent split restriction category
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Proof.

By direct inspection, one can show that the
2
-category
\mathsf{SRSTR}
admits inserters and equifiers. Therefore, by Theorem
5.23
,
\mathsf{SRSTR}
admits the construction of vector fields. By unwrapping this construction, one finds out that the tangentad of vector fields of a tangent split restriction category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is precisely
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.
∎

Tangent restriction categories: a general approach

General tangent restriction categories are not an example of tangentads. However, thanks to Proposition
[
18
, Lemma 4.36]
, every tangent restriction category embeds into a tangent split restriction category, which, by
[
18
, Proposition 4.35]
, is a tangentad in the
2
-category
\mathsf{SRSTR}
of split restriction categories.

In this section, we introduce a general procedure for extending the formal theory of vector fields to tangent-like concepts which might fail to be tangentads. Thus, we apply this general construction to the case of tangent restriction categories.

Definition 5.30
.

A
pullback-extension context
consists of two
2
-categories
{\mathbf{K}}
, and
{\mathbf{J}}
, two
2
-functors

\displaystyle\Xi

\displaystyle\colon{\mathbf{J}}\leftrightarrows\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})\colon\mathsf{Inc}

and a natural
2
-transformation

\displaystyle\eta_{\mathbb{X}}

\displaystyle\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathsf{Inc}(\Xi(\mathbb{X}))

for
\mathbb{X}\in{\mathbf{J}}
.

The functors

\displaystyle\mathsf{Inc}\colon\mathsf{TNG}(\mathsf{SRSTR})\cong\mathsf{TNGSRSTR}\to\mathsf{TNGRSTR}

\displaystyle\Xi=\mathsf{Split}_{R}\colon\mathsf{TNGRSTR}\to\mathsf{TNGSRSTR}\cong\mathsf{TNG}(\mathsf{SRSTR})

where
\mathsf{Inc}
is the inclusion of
\mathsf{TNGSRSTR}
into
\mathsf{TNGRSTR}
as a sub
2
-category and
\mathsf{Split}_{R}
sends each tangent restriction category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
into the tangent split restriction category
(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbb{X}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbb{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)
obtained by formally splitting the restriction idempotents of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, together with the natural
2
-transformation

\displaystyle\eta_{(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})}\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbb{X}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbb{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)=\mathsf{Inc}(\mathsf{Split}_{R}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))

forms a pullback-extension context. In particular, the objects of
(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbb{X}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathbb{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)
are pairs
(A,e)
formed by an object
A
of
\mathbb{X}
and a restriction idempotent
e=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mue\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
of
\mathbb{X}
. Thus,
\varphi
sends each
A\in(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
to
(A,\mathsf{id}_{A})
.

Consider a pullback-extension context
({\mathbf{K}},{\mathbf{J}};\Xi,\mathsf{Inc},\Xi;\eta)
. Assume also that
{\mathbf{K}}
admits the construction of vector fields. We want to extend the construction of vector fields on
{\mathbf{J}}
, by pulling back along
\eta
. In particular, let us assume that the following
2
-pullback diagram exists in
{\mathbf{J}}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Inc}(\mathsf{VF}(\Xi(\mathbb{X}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Inc}(\Xi(\mathbb{X}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{VF}(\eta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{U}_{\mathbb{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Inc}(\mathsf{U}_{\Xi(\mathbb{X})})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

(5.2)

Definition 5.31
.

Let
({\mathbf{K}},{\mathbf{J}};\mathsf{Inc},\Xi;\eta)
be a pullback-extension context. An object
\mathbb{X}
of
{\mathbf{J}}
admits the
extended construction of vector fields
(w.r.t. to the pullback-extension context) if
\Xi(\mathbb{X})
admits the construction of vector fields in
{\mathbf{K}}
and the
2
-pullback diagram of Equation (
5.2
) exists in
{\mathbf{J}}
. In this scenario, the
object of vector fields
(w.r.t. to the pullback-extension context) of
\mathbb{X}
is the object
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X})
of
{\mathbf{J}}
, namely, the
2
-pullback of
\mathsf{Inc}(\mathsf{U}_{\Xi(\mathbb{X})})
along
\eta
.

We have already shown that the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNGRSTR}
of tangent restriction categories admits a pullback-extension context, whose base
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
is the
2
-category
\mathsf{RSTR}
of restriction categories and the natural
2
-transformation
\eta
is the inclusion of each tangent restriction category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
into its formal split completion
\mathsf{Inc}(\mathsf{Split}_{R}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))
. The goal is to use this context to extend the construction of vector fields to general tangent (non-necessarily split) restriction categories. Let us start by guessing what the object of vector fields of a tangent restriction category would look like.

In the previous section, we showed that the tangentad of vector fields of a tangent split restriction tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is the tangent split restriction category
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
whose objects are pairs
(M,v)
formed by an object
M
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
together with a total map
v\colon M\to\mathrm{T}M
which is a section of the projection, namely,
p\circ v=\mathsf{id}_{M}
and morphisms
f\colon(M,v)\to(N,u)
are morphisms
f\colon M\to N
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
between the underlying objects of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
which are compatible with
v
and
u
, namely,
u\circ f=\mathrm{T}f\circ v
, and whose restriction idempotent is
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muf\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
. We showed that each restriction idempotent splits in
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Now, consider a generic tangent restriction category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and let us unwrap the definition of
\mathsf{VF}(\mathsf{Split}_{R}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))
. The objects of
\mathsf{VF}(\mathsf{Split}_{R}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))
are pairs
(M,v)
formed by an object
M
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
together with a map
v\colon M\to\mathrm{T}M
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, which satisfies the following condition:

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muv\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\circ v=v

A morphism
f\colon(M,v)\to(M^{\prime},v^{\prime})
of
\mathsf{VF}(\mathsf{Split}_{R}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))
consists of a morphism
f\colon M\to M^{\prime}
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
which commutes with the vector fields and the restriction idempotents, namely:

\displaystyle v^{\prime}\circ f=\mathrm{T}f\circ v

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muv^{\prime}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\circ f=f=f\circ\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muv\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

The tangent bundle functor sends each
(M,v)
to
(\mathrm{T}M,c\circ\mathrm{T}v)
.

Now, consider a tangent (non-necessarily split) restriction category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and define
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
to be the full subcategory of
\mathsf{VF}(\mathsf{Split}_{R}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))
spanned by the objects
(M,v)
where
v\colon M\to\mathrm{T}M
is total in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, namely,
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muv\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mathsf{id}_{M}
.

Lemma 5.32
.

Let
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
be a tangent restriction category. The subcategory
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
\mathsf{VF}(\mathsf{Split}_{R}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))
spanned by the objects
(M,v)
where
v
is total in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is a tangent restriction category.

Proof.

For starters, notice that
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
inherits from
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
a restriction structure, where the restriction idempotent of
f\colon(M,v)\to(M^{\prime},v^{\prime})
is
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muf\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
. Consider
\mathsf{Split}_{R}(\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))
, whose objects are triples
(M,v;e)
formed by an object
(M,v)
of
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and an idempotent
e=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mue\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
, satisfying
v\circ e=\mathrm{T}e\circ v
. A morphism
f\colon(M,v;e)\to(M^{\prime},v^{\prime};e^{\prime})
of
\mathsf{Split}_{R}(\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))
is a morphism
f\colon(M,v)\to(M^{\prime},v^{\prime})
of
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, such that
e^{\prime}\circ f=f=f\circ e
and whose restriction idempotent is
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muf\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
. Define the following functor

\displaystyle\mathsf{Split}_{R}(\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))\to\mathsf{VF}(\mathsf{Split}_{R}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))

which sends each
(M,v;e)
to
(M,v\circ e)
and each morphism
f\colon(M,v;e)\to(M^{\prime},v^{\prime};e^{\prime})
to
f\colon(M,v\circ e)\to(M^{\prime},v^{\prime}\circ e^{\prime})
. Thus,
\mathsf{Split}_{R}(\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))
can be seen as a subcategory of
\mathsf{VF}(\mathsf{Split}_{R}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))
. By restricting the tangent split restriction category of
\mathsf{VF}(\mathsf{Split}_{R}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))
to
\mathsf{Split}_{R}(\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))
we obtain a tangent split restriction category. However,
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is a tangent restriction category if and only if
\mathsf{Split}_{R}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is a tangent split restriction category. This proves that
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is a tangent restriction category.
∎

We can finally prove the main theorem of this section.

Theorem 5.33
.

Consider the pullback-extension context
(\mathsf{RSTR},\mathsf{TNGRSTR};\mathsf{Inc},\mathsf{Split}_{R};\eta)
of tangent restriction categories. The
2
-category
\mathsf{TNGRSTR}
admits the extended construction of vector fields with respect to this pullback-extension context. Moreover, the object of vector fields of a tangent restriction category is the tangent restriction category
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Proof.

Consider the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Inc}(\mathsf{VF}(\mathsf{Split}_{R}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Inc}(\mathsf{Split}_{R}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{VF}(\eta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Inc}(\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{Split}_{R}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

where
\mathsf{VF}(\eta)
sends each pair
(M,v)
to
(M,v)
and each morphism
f\colon(M,v)\to(M^{\prime},v^{\prime})
to
f
and
\mathsf{U}
forgets the vector field, namely,
\mathsf{U}(M,v)=M
. Consider also another tangent restriction category
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
and two tangent restriction functors making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Inc}(\mathsf{VF}(\mathsf{Split}_{R}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Inc}(\mathsf{Split}_{R}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G,\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F,\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{VF}(\eta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Inc}(\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{Split}_{R}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

In particular, let
GA=(FA,v_{FA})
for each
A\in\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
. By functoriality,
G(\mathsf{id}_{A})=\mathsf{id}_{(FA,v_{FA})}=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muv_{FA}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
, where we used that the identity morphisms in
\mathsf{VF}(\mathsf{Split}_{R}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))
are the idempotents, namely,
\mathsf{id}_{(M,v)}=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muv\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
. However, we can also compute:

\displaystyle\mathsf{id}_{FA}=\eta(F(\mathsf{id}_{A}))=\mathsf{Inc}(\mathsf{U})(G(\mathsf{id}_{A}))=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muv_{F}A\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

Therefore, the vector field
v_{FA}\colon FA\to\mathrm{T}FA
must be total in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Thus, we can define a functor

\displaystyle(H,\gamma)

\displaystyle\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

which sends each
A
to
(FA,v_{FA})
. It is not hard to see that this functor extends to a tangent morphism and that it is the unique functor which makes the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Inc}(\mathsf{VF}(\mathsf{Split}_{R}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Inc}(\mathsf{Split}_{R}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H,\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G,\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F,\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{VF}(\eta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Inc}(\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{Split}_{R}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

We leave the reader to complete the proof that
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is the
2
-pullback of
\mathsf{Inc}(\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{Split}_{R}})
along
\eta
.
∎

6
The formal theory of differential objects

In every Cartesian tangent category, one can define a suitable class of objects, called
differential objects
, which mimic the properties and structure of Euclidean spaces in differential geometry. This section aims to formalize this notion in the context of tangentads.

6.1
Differential objects in tangent category theory

Concretely, a differential object in a Cartesian tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is a four-tuple
(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},{\hat{p}}_{A})
consisting of an object
A
of
\mathbb{X}
and three morphisms, so defined:

​
[
Zero morphism
]
:

A morphism
\zeta_{A}\colon\ast\to A
;

​
[
Sum morphism
]
:

A morphism
\sigma_{A}\colon A\times A\to A
;

such that the triple
(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A})
is a commutative monoid in the Cartesian category
\mathbb{X}
. Moreover:

​
[
Differential projection
]
:

A morphism
{\hat{p}}_{A}\colon\mathrm{T}A\to A
;

which satisfies the following conditions:

(a)

Additivity 1. The morphism
{\hat{p}}_{A}\colon(\mathrm{T}A,\mathrm{T}\zeta_{A},\mathrm{T}\sigma_{A})\to(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A})
is a morphism of commutative monoids, where we identify
\mathrm{T}(A\times A)
with
\mathrm{T}A\times\mathrm{T}A
and
\mathrm{T}\ast
with
\ast
. Concretely, this corresponds to the commutativity of the following diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{p}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\zeta_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{A}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A\times\mathrm{T}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{p}}_{A}\times{\hat{p}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\sigma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{p}}_{A}}

(b)

Additivity 2. The morphism
(!,{\hat{p}}_{A})\colon(p\colon\mathrm{T}A\to A,z_{A},s_{A})\to(!\colon A\to\ast,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A})
is an additive bundle morphism. Concretely, this corresponds to the commutativity of the following diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{p}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{A}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{2}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{p}}_{A}\times_{!}{\hat{p}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{p}}_{A}}

(c)

Linearity. The following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}{\hat{p}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{p}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{p}}_{A}}

(d)

Universality. The following is a product diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{p}}_{A}}

The commutative monoid structure of a differential object corresponds to that one of the vector space underlying a Euclidean space, where
\zeta_{A}\colon\ast\to A
sets the zero vector, while
\sigma_{A}\colon A\times A\to A
is the sum of vectors. The differential projection
{\hat{p}}_{A}\colon\mathrm{T}A\to A
corresponds to the canonical map which identifies the tangent space
\mathrm{T}_{x}A
at each point
x
of a Euclidean space
A
to the space
A
itself, by sending a tangent vector
v
of
\mathrm{T}_{x}A
to the point
x+v
, where
x
is intepreted both as a point of
A
and as a vector.

Differential objects of a Cartesian tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
form a Cartesian tangent category, denoted by
\mathsf{DO}^{\ast}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, so defined:

​
[
Objects
]
:

An object of
\mathsf{DO}^{\ast}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is a differential object
(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},{\hat{p}}_{A})
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
;

​
[
Morphisms
]
:

A morphism of
\mathsf{DO}^{\ast}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is a morphism
f\colon A\to B
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
between the underlying objects of two differential objects;

​
[
Tangent bundle functor
]
:

The tangent bundle functor sends a differential object
(A,\zeta_{,}\sigma_{A},{\hat{p}}_{A})
to the differential object
(\mathrm{T}A,\mathrm{T}\zeta_{A},\mathrm{T}\sigma_{A},{\hat{p}}_{\mathrm{T}A})
, where we identified
\mathrm{T}(A\times A)
with
\mathrm{T}A\times\mathrm{T}A
and
\mathrm{T}\ast
with
\ast
, and whose differential projection is defined as follows:

\displaystyle{\hat{p}}_{\mathrm{T}A}\colon\mathrm{T}^{2}A\xrightarrow{c}\mathrm{T}^{2}A\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}{\hat{p}}_{A}}\mathrm{T}A

​
[
structural natural transformations
]
:

The structural natural transformations of
\mathsf{DO}^{\ast}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
are the same as the ones of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Such a tangent category carries the structure of a Cartesian differential category whose differential combinator sends a morphism
f\colon A\to B
of differential objects to its derivative:

\displaystyle D[f]\colon A\times A\cong\mathrm{T}A\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}f}\mathrm{T}B\xrightarrow{{\hat{p}}_{B}}B

We suggest the reader consult the original paper
[
5
]
for details on this construction and
[
3
]
to learn more about Cartesian differential categories.

In
\mathsf{DO}^{\ast}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, a
linear morphism
, is a morphism
f\colon A\to B
between differential objects, which commutes with the differential projections, namely:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{p}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{p}}_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Differential objects and linear morphisms form a Cartesian sub-tangent category of
\mathsf{DO}^{\ast}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, denoted by
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. The operation
\mathsf{DO}^{\ast}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
extends to a
2
-endofunctor, as discussed by the next lemma, which was already presented in
[
5
, Section 4.1]
.

Lemma 6.1
.

There is a
2
-endofunctor

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}^{\ast}\colon\mathsf{CTNGCAT}_{\cong}\to\mathsf{CTNGCAT}_{\cong}

on the category of Cartesian tangent categories and strong tangent morphisms which preserve finite products, which sends a Cartesian tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
to the Cartesian tangent category
\mathsf{DO}^{\ast}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of differential objects of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and that sends a strong tangent morphism
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
which preserves finite products to the strong tangent morphism which sends a differential object
(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},{\hat{p}}_{A})
to the differential object
(FA,F\zeta_{A},F\sigma_{A},{\hat{p}}_{FA})
, whose differential projection is defined as follows:

\displaystyle{\hat{p}}_{FA}\colon\mathrm{T}FA\xrightarrow{\alpha^{-1}}F\mathrm{T}^{\prime}A\xrightarrow{F{\hat{p}}_{A}}FA

Furthermore,
\mathsf{DO}
restricts to linear morphisms, yielding another
2
-functor:

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}\colon\mathsf{CTNGCAT}_{\cong}\to\mathsf{CTNGCAT}_{\cong}

Proof.

We suggest the reader consult
[
5
, Section 4.1]
.
∎

6.2
The universal property of differential objects

This section aims to characterize the correct universal property of the tangent category
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of differential objects and linear morphisms of a Cartesian tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. For starters, consider the forgetful functor
\mathsf{U}\colon\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
which sends a differential object
A
to its underlying object of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Since
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is Cartesian, there are two (strong) tangent morphisms

\displaystyle\ast

\displaystyle\colon\mathds{1}\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\times(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

sending the unique object of the terminal tangent category
\mathds{1}
to the (chosen) terminal object
\ast
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and sending a pair of objects
(A,B)
to their Cartesian product
A\times B
, respectively. Therefore, we can define the following natural transformations

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})}\colon\ast(!(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A}))=\ast\xrightarrow{\zeta_{A}}A=\mathsf{U}(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})}\colon\times(\langle\mathsf{U},\mathsf{U}\rangle(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A}))=A\times A\xrightarrow{\sigma_{A}}A=\mathsf{U}(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})

where
!\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to\ast
is the terminal morphism, namely, the constant functor. However, as shown by Lemma
3.3
and by Proposition
3.4
, the functors
\ast\>\circ\>!
and
\times\circ\langle\mathsf{U},\mathsf{U}\rangle
are precisely the terminal object
\top
and the Cartesian product
\mathsf{U}\Box\mathsf{U}
in the Cartesian Hom-tangent category
[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
.

Lemma 6.2
.

The forgetful functor
\mathsf{U}\colon\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
equipped with the natural transformations
\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{S}
, is a commutative monoid in the Cartesian Hom-tangent category
[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
.

Proof.

First, we need to prove that
\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{S}
are tangent natural transformations. Thanks to
[
8
, Propositions 3.7 and 2.16]
, linear morphisms of differential objects are additive, namely they preserve the commutative monoid structures. In particular, this implies that
\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{S}
are natural transformations. To prove they are compatible with the tangent structure, notice that, by definition of the tangent structure on
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{T}^{\mathsf{DO}}(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})}=\mathrm{T}\zeta_{A}=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(\mathcal{Z}_{(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})})

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{T}^{\mathsf{DO}}(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})}=\mathrm{T}\sigma_{A}=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(\mathcal{S}_{(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})})

Thus,
\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{S}
are compatible with the tangent structure. To prove that
(\mathsf{U},\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S})
defines a commutative monoid, one simply uses that each
(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A})
constitutes a commutative monoid.
∎

The next step is to show that the commutative monoid
(\mathsf{U},\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S})
comes with a differential projection. Define:

\displaystyle\mathcal{\hat{P}}_{(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})}\colon\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\mathsf{U}(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})=\mathrm{T}A\xrightarrow{\hat{p}_{A}}A=\mathsf{U}(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})

By the linearity of the morphisms in
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
,
\mathcal{\hat{P}}
is natural. Furthermore, since

\displaystyle\mathcal{\hat{P}}_{\mathrm{T}^{\mathsf{DO}}(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})}=\mathrm{T}\hat{p}_{A}\circ c

and
c^{2}=\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}^{2}}
, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\circ\mathsf{U}\circ\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{2}\circ\mathsf{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{2}\circ\mathsf{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{U}\circ\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}\circ\mathsf{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{\hat{P}}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\mathsf{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\mathcal{\hat{P}}}

Thus,
\mathcal{\hat{P}}
is a tangent natural transformation.

Definition 6.3
.

A
pointwise differential object
consists of a differential object
(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})
in a Hom-tangent category
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
such that, the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(G,\beta)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(G,\beta)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(G,\beta)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mup\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{p}}

is a pointwise product diagram in
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
.

Proposition 6.4
.

The forgetful functor
\mathsf{U}\colon\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
equipped with the tangent natural transformations
\mathcal{Z}
,
\mathcal{S}
, and
\mathcal{\hat{P}}
defines a pointwise differential object in the Cartesian Hom-tangent category
[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
.

Proof.

In Lemma
6.2
we proved that
(\mathsf{U},\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S})
is a commutative monoid in
[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
. The equational axioms required for
\mathcal{\hat{P}}
to be a differential projection of
(\mathsf{U},\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S})
, are a consequence of
\mathcal{\hat{P}}_{(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})}
being the differential projection of each differential object
(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})
. Notice that the compatibility with the vertical lift is also a consequence of the compatibility between
l_{A}\colon\mathrm{T}A\to\mathrm{T}^{2}A
and
\hat{p}_{A}
. Finally, to prove the universal property of
\mathcal{\hat{P}}
, notice that limits on the target tangent category induce pointwise limits in the Hom-tangent category.
∎

To characterize the correct universal property enjoyed by the pointwise differential object of Proposition
6.4
, for starters, let us unwrap the definition of a differential object in each Hom-tangent category
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime}]
, where
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
and
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime})
are Cartesian tangent categories:

​
[
Base object
]
:

The base object is a lax tangent morphism
(G,\beta)\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime})
;

​
[
Zero morphism
]
:

The zero morphism consists of a tangent natural transformation:

\displaystyle\zeta\colon\top\to(G,\beta)

​
[
Sum morphism
]
:

The sum morphism consists of a tangent natural transformation:

\displaystyle\sigma\colon(G,\beta)\>\Box\>(G,\beta)\to(G,\beta)

​
[
Differential projection
]
:

The differential projection consists of a tangent natural transformation:

\displaystyle\hat{p}\colon\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(G,\beta)\to(G,\beta)

satisfying the axioms of a pointwise differential object. A morphism of differential objects in the Hom-tangent category
[\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}]
from a pointwise differential object
(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})
to a pointwise differential object
(G^{\prime},\beta^{\prime};\zeta^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},\hat{p}^{\prime})
consists of a tangent natural transformation
\varphi\colon(G,\beta)\to(G^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
which commutes with the differential structures.

For each tangent category
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
, a pointwise differential object
(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})
in the Hom-tangent category
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime\prime}]
induces a functor

\displaystyle\Gamma_{(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})}\colon[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime}]\to\mathsf{DO}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime\prime}]

which sends a lax tangent morphism
(H,\gamma)\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime})
to the pointwise differential object

\displaystyle\Gamma_{(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})}(H,\gamma)\>\colon\!=((G,\beta)\circ(H,\gamma);\zeta_{(H,\gamma)},\sigma_{(H,\gamma)},\hat{p}_{(H,\gamma)})

in the Hom-tangent category
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime\prime}]
. Concretely, the base object of
\Gamma_{(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})}(H,\gamma)
is the lax tangent morphism

\displaystyle(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{(H,\gamma)}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime})\xrightarrow{(G,\beta)}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime\prime})

while the zero, the sum, and the differential projection of
\Gamma_{(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})}(H,\gamma)
are the natural transformations:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H,\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\top}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H,\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G,\beta)\Box(G,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H,\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(G,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{p}}

As observed for vector fields, this construction is functorial and extends to a tangent morphism.

Lemma 6.5
.

A differential object
(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})
in the Hom-tangent category
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime\prime}]
induces a lax tangent morphism

\displaystyle\Gamma_{(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})}\colon[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime}]\to\mathsf{DO}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime\prime}]

for each tangentad
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
. Furthermore,
\Gamma_{(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})}
is natural in
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
and it is strong (strict) when
(G,\beta)
is strong (strict).

Proof.

The proof of this lemma is fairly similar to that of Lemma
5.3
.
∎

In particular, by Lemma
6.5
, the differential object
(\mathsf{U};\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})
of Proposition
6.4
induces a strict tangent natural transformation

\displaystyle\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}\colon[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})]\to\mathsf{DO}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]

natural in
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
. In the following, we denote
\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}
simply by
\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}
.

We want to prove that
\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}
is invertible. Consider another tangent category
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
together with a differential object
(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})
in the Hom-tangent category
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
. For every
A\in\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
, the tuple

\displaystyle\left(GA,\zeta_{A}^{\prime}\colon\ast\to GA,\sigma_{A}^{\prime}\colon GA\times GA\to GA,\hat{p}_{A}^{\prime}\colon GA\to\mathrm{T}GA\right)

is a differential object in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Therefore, we can define a functor

\displaystyle\Lambda[G,\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}]\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

which sends an object
A
of
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
to the differential object
(GA,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})
, and a morphism
f\colon A\to B
to
Gf
. By the naturality of
\zeta
,
\sigma
, and
\hat{p}
,

\displaystyle Gf\colon(GA,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})\to(GB,\zeta_{B},\sigma_{B},\hat{p}_{B})

becomes a linear morphism of differential objects of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Furthermore,
\Lambda[G,\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}]
comes with a distributive law

\displaystyle\Lambda[\beta]\colon\Lambda[G,\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}](\mathrm{T}^{\prime}A)=(G\mathrm{T}^{\prime}A,\zeta_{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}A},\sigma_{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}A},\hat{p}_{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}A})\xrightarrow{\beta_{A}}(\mathrm{T}GA,\mathrm{T}\zeta_{A}^{\prime},\mathrm{T}\sigma_{A},c_{A}\circ\mathrm{T}\hat{p}_{A})=\mathrm{T}^{\mathsf{DO}}(\Lambda[G,\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}](A))

which is a morphism of differential objects since each of the structural morphisms
\zeta
,
\sigma
, and
\hat{p}
are tangent natural transformations and thus compatible with the distributive law
\beta
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Given a lax tangent morphism
(G,\beta)\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and a differential structure
(\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})
for
(G,\beta)
in the Hom-tangent category
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
, the functor
\Lambda[G,\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}]
together with the distributive law
\Lambda[\beta]
defines a lax tangent morphism:

\displaystyle\Lambda[G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}]\colon(\Lambda[G,\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}],\Lambda[\beta])\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

Proof.

In the previous discussion, we defined
\Lambda[G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}]
as the lax tangent morphism which picks out the differential object
(GA;\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})
for each
A\in\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
and whose distributive law is induced by
\beta
. In particular, the naturality of the structure morphisms
\zeta
,
\sigma
, and
\hat{p}
makes the functor
\Lambda[G,\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}]
well-defined, the compatibility of the structure morphisms and
\beta
makes
\Lambda[\beta]
into a morphism of differential objects, and the compatibility of
\beta
with the tangent structures makes
\Lambda[G,\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}]
into a lax tangent morphism.
∎

We can now prove the universal property of differential objects, which is the main result of this section.

Theorem 6.7
.

The differential object
(\mathsf{U},\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})
of Proposition
6.4
is universal. Concretely, the induced strict tangent natural transformation

\displaystyle\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}\colon[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})]\to\mathsf{DO}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]

makes the functor

\displaystyle\mathsf{TNGCAT}^{\mathsf{op}}\xrightarrow{[-\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]}\mathsf{TNGCAT}^{\mathsf{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{DO}^{\mathsf{op}}}\mathsf{TNGCAT}^{\mathsf{op}}

which sends a tangent category
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
to the tangent category
\mathsf{DO}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
, into a corepresentable functor. In particular,
(\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}
is invertible.

Proof.

Consider a tangent category
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
and a lax tangent morphism
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, which sends each
A\in\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
to the differential object
(FA,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})
. We want to compare
(F,\alpha)
with
\Lambda[\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}(F,\alpha)]
. Concretely,
\Lambda[\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}(F,\alpha)]
sends an object
A
of
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
to the differential objects
\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}(F,\alpha)_{A}
. However, this corresponds to the differential object
(FA,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})
. Given a morphism
\varphi\colon(F,\alpha)\to(F^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
of
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})]

\displaystyle\varphi_{A}\colon(FA,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})\to(F^{\prime}A,\zeta^{\prime}_{A},\sigma^{\prime}_{A},\hat{p}^{\prime}_{A})

which coincides with the morphism
\Lambda[\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}(\varphi)]
, since:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}(\varphi)_{A}=FA\xrightarrow{\varphi}F^{\prime}A

Conversely, consider a differential object
(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})
of the Hom-tangent category
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
. Thus,
\Lambda[G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}]
sends each
A
to the differential object
(GA,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})
. Therefore,
\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}(\Lambda[G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}])
coincides with
(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})
. Similarly, for a given morphism
\varphi(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})\to(G^{\prime},\beta^{\prime};\zeta^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},\hat{p}^{\prime})
of differential objects,
\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}(\Lambda[\varphi])
coincides with
\varphi
.

Finally, it is not hard to see that the functor

\displaystyle\Lambda\colon\mathsf{DO}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]\to[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})]

extends to a strict tangent morphism, since both
\Lambda[\mathrm{T}^{\mathsf{DO}}(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})]
and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(\Lambda[G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}])
send each
A\in\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
to the differential object

\displaystyle(\mathrm{T}GA,\mathrm{T}\zeta_{A},\mathrm{T}\sigma_{A},c_{A}\circ\mathrm{T}\hat{p}_{A})

Therefore,
\Lambda
inverts
\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}
.
∎

Theorem
6.7
establishes the correct universal property of the differential objects construction. Thanks to this result, we can finally introduce the notion of differential objects in the formal context of tangentads.

Definition 6.8
.

A Cartesian tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
in a
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}

admits the construction of differential objects
if there exists a Cartesian tangentad
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
{\mathbf{K}}
together with a (strict) tangent morphism
\mathsf{U}\colon\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and a pointwise differential object
(\mathsf{U};\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})
in the Hom-tangent category
[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
such that the induced tangent natural transformation
\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}
of Lemma
6.5
is invertible. The pointwise differential object
(\mathsf{U};\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})
is called the
universal pointwise differential object
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is called the
tangentad of differential objects
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Definition 6.9
.

A Cartesian
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}

admits the construction of differential objects
provided that every Cartesian tangentad of
{\mathbf{K}}
admits the construction of differential objects.

We can now rephrase Theorem
6.7
as follows.

Corollary 6.10
.

The
2
-category
\mathsf{CAT}
of categories admits the construction of differential objects and the tangentad of differential objects of a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
\mathsf{CAT}
is the tangent category
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of differential objects of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

To ensure that Definitions
6.8
and
6.9
are well-posed, one requires the tangentad of differential objects of a given tangentad to be unique. The next proposition establishes that such a construction is defined uniquely up to a unique isomorphism.

Proposition 6.11
.

If a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
admits the construction of differential objects, the tangentad of differential objects
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is unique up to a unique isomorphism which extends to an isomorphism of the corresponding universal differential objects of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Proof.

The proof of this proposition is fairly similar to that of Proposition
5.9
.
∎

6.3
The formal structures of differential objects

In Section
6.1
, we recalled that (a) differential objects of a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
form a new tangent category
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
; (b) the assigment which sends a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
to
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is
2
-functorial.

In this section, we recover the
2
-functoriality of the construction of differential objects in the context of tangentads. For starters, notice that, in the context of tangentads, (a) is a built-in property of the definition of the tangentad of differential objects
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, since
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is, by definition, a tangentad.

In the following,
{\mathbf{K}}
denotes a
2
-category which admits the construction of differential objects.

The functoriality of the construction of differential objects

As proved in Lemma
6.1
, there is a
2
-functor

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}\colon\mathsf{CTNGCAT}_{\cong}\to\mathsf{CTNGCAT}_{\cong}

which sends a Cartesian tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
to the Cartesian tangent category
\mathsf{DO}(\otimes,\mathbb{T})
of differential objects of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

In this section, we show that the construction of differential objects is
2
-functorial in the
2
-category of tangentads of
{\mathbf{K}}
. Consider a strong tangent morphism
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
between two Cartesian tangentads of
{\mathbf{K}}
which preserves finite products. It is not hard to prove that

\displaystyle[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]\xrightarrow{[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>F,\alpha]}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}]

is a strong tangent morphism which preserves finite products. Thus,
\mathsf{DO}
sends
[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>F,\alpha]
to a strong tangent morphism

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]\xrightarrow{\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>F,\alpha]}\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}]\cong[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})]

where we used the universal property of differential objects to identify
\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}]
with
[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})]
. In particular, the universal differential object
\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}}
is sent by this tangent morphism to a tangent morphism

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}(F,\alpha)\colon\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})

Now, consider a
2
-morphism
\varphi\colon(F,\alpha)\Rightarrow(G,\beta)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
and define the tangent natural transformation:

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\varphi]\colon\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>F,\alpha]\Rightarrow\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>G,\beta]

Thus, by evaluating such a transformation on the universal differential object and by employing the universal property of differential objects, we obtain a
2
-morphism:

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}(\varphi)\colon\mathsf{DO}(F,\alpha)\Rightarrow\mathsf{DO}(G,\beta)

Proposition 6.12
.

There is a
2
-functor

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}\colon\mathsf{CTNG}({\mathbf{K}})_{\cong}\to\mathsf{CTNG}({\mathbf{K}})_{\cong}

which sends a Cartesian tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
{\mathbf{K}}
to the tangentad of differential objects
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. In particular, for each strong tangent morphism
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
, the tangent morphism
\mathsf{DO}(F,\alpha)\colon\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
is also strong.

Proof.

Consider a strong tangent morphism
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
between two Cartesian tangentads which preserves finite products. As previously discussed, let
\mathsf{DO}(F,\alpha)
the tangent morphism which correspond to the universal differential object
\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}}
via the functor

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]\xrightarrow{\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>F,\alpha]}\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}]\cong[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})]

The underlying tangent morphism of
\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>F,\alpha](\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})
is simply given by

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\xrightarrow{\mathsf{U}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\xrightarrow{(F,\alpha)}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

Given a
2
-morphism
\varphi\colon(F,\alpha)\Rightarrow(G,\beta)
,
\mathsf{DO}(\varphi)
is the
2
-morphism corresponding to the tangent natural transformation

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\varphi]\colon\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>F,\alpha]\Rightarrow\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>G,\beta]

evaluated at the universal differential object.
∎

6.4
Applications

In Section
2.5
, we listed some examples of tangentads. In this section, we compute the construction of differential objects for each of these examples. Unfortunately, the construction of differential objects cannot be obtained by means of PIE limits as it was done for vector fields. The reason is that the universal property enjoyed by the differential projection of a differential object is a non-equational axiom, and PIE limits can only construct algebraic theories. Therefore, in this section, we follow a direct approach to construct differential objects for the different tangentads.

Tangent monads

The first step to computing the construction of differential objects for tangent monads is to understand what a Cartesian tangent monad is, namely, a Cartesian object in the
2
-category of tangent monads (or equivalently, a tangentad in the
2
-category of Cartesian objects in
\mathsf{MND}
).

A
Cartesian
tangent monad consists of a Cartesian tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
together with a tangent monad
(S,\alpha)
on
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
which preserves finite products. In general, we do not expect
(S,\alpha)
to lift to the categories of differential objects of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
unless the distributive law
\alpha
is invertible. We call a
strong
Cartesian tangent monad a Cartesian monad whose underlying tangent morphism
(S,\alpha)
is strong.

Lemma 6.13
.

A strong Cartesian tangent monad
(S,\alpha)
on a Cartesian tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
lifts to a Cartesian tangent monad on the Cartesian tangent category
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of differential objects of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Proof.

Strong tangent morphisms send differential objects to differential objects. In particular,
S(A,\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})
is the differential object
(SA,S\zeta,S\sigma,S\hat{p}\circ\alpha^{-1})
. Furthermore, by naturality and thanks to the compatibility with
\alpha
, the unit
\eta
and the multiplication
\mu
of
S
are morphisms of differential objects.
∎

Theorem 6.14
.

In the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNGMND}
of tangent monads, each strong Cartesian tangent monad
(S,\alpha)
on a Cartesian tangent category admits the construction of differential objects. Moreover, the tangentad of differential objects of
(S,\alpha)
is the Cartesian tangent monad
\mathsf{DO}(S,\alpha)
on
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Proof.

In Lemma
6.13
, we already proved that
\mathsf{DO}(S,\alpha)
defines a tangent monad on the tangent category
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of differential objects of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Now, we want to prove that
(\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}),\mathsf{DO}(S,\alpha))
represents the tangentad of differential objects of the tangent monad
(S,\alpha)
. Consider a tangent category
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
equipped with a tangent monad
(S^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
. A differential object in the Hom-tangent category
[(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime};S^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\>|\>(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};S,\alpha)]
is a lax tangent morphism
(G,\beta)\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
equipped with a distributive law
\tau\colon S\circ G\to G\circ S^{\prime}
, compatible with the monad structures and the distributive laws
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
. Furthermore,
(G,\beta;\tau)
comes equipped with tangent natural transformations

\displaystyle\zeta_{A}\colon\ast\to GA

\displaystyle\sigma_{A}\colon GA\times GA\to GA

\displaystyle\hat{p}_{A}\colon\mathrm{T}GA\to GA

which make each
GA
into a differential object of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Moreover,
\zeta
,
\sigma
,
\hat{p}
must also be natural transformations of monads, namely, they must be compatible with
\tau
. Thus, the lax tangent morphism

\displaystyle(H,\gamma)\colon

\displaystyle(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

which sends each
A\in(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
to
(GA,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})
comes equipped with a distributive law
\theta

\displaystyle\theta_{A}\colon\mathsf{DO}(S,\alpha)(HA)=(SGA,S\zeta_{A},S\sigma_{A},\alpha^{-1}\circ S\hat{p}_{A})\xrightarrow{\gamma_{A}}(GS^{\prime}A,GS^{\prime}\zeta_{A},GS^{\prime}\sigma_{A},{\alpha^{\prime}}^{-1}\circ GS^{\prime}\hat{p}_{A})=H(S^{\prime}A)

which makes
(H,\gamma)
into a morphisms of tangent monads. We let the reader prove that
(H,\gamma;\tau)
is the unique such morphism.
∎

Tangent fibrations

In this section, we consider the construction of differential objects for tangent fibrations. First, let us unwrap the definition of a Cartesian tangent fibration, namely, a Cartesian object in the
2
-category of tangent fibrations.

A
Cartesian
tangent fibration consists of a tangent fibration
\Pi\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
between two Cartesian tangent categories
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
and
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
whose underlying functor strictly preserves finite products, namely,
\Pi
sends the terminal object of
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
to the (chosen) terminal object of
\mathbb{X}
and sends each Cartesian product
E\times F
of
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
to the (chosen) product
\Pi(E)\times\Pi(F)
of
\mathbb{X}
. Moreover, for each pair of morphisms
f\colon A\to C
and
g\colon B\to D
of
\mathbb{X}
, the Cartesian product

\displaystyle\varphi_{f}\times\varphi_{g}\colon f^{\ast}E\times g^{\ast}F\to E\times F

of the (cloven) Cartesian lifts
\varphi_{f}\colon f^{\ast}E\to E
and
\varphi_{g}\colon g^{\ast}F\to F
is the (cloven) Cartesian lift
\varphi_{f\times g}
of
f\times g
onto
E\times F
.

Lemma 6.15
.

Consider a (cloven) Cartesian tangent fibration
\Pi\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
between two Cartesian tangent categories. The strict tangent morphism

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}(\Pi)\colon\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

which sends a differential object
(E,\zeta_{E},\sigma_{E},\hat{p}_{E})
to the differential object
(\Pi(E),\Pi(\zeta_{E}),\Pi(\sigma_{E}),\Pi(\hat{p}_{E}))
is a (cloven) Cartesian tangent fibration.

Proof.

Consider a (linear) morphism
f\colon(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})\to(B,\zeta_{B},\sigma_{B},\hat{p}_{B})
of differential objects in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and let
(E,\zeta_{E},\sigma_{E},\hat{p}_{E})
be a differential object of
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
such that
\Pi(E,\zeta_{E},\sigma_{E},\hat{p}_{E})=(B,\zeta_{B},\sigma_{B},\hat{p}_{B})
. Using the universal property of the Cartesian lift
\varphi_{f}\colon f^{\ast}E\to E
we can define the morphisms

\displaystyle f^{\ast}\zeta_{E}\colon\ast\to f^{\ast}E

\displaystyle f^{\sigma}_{E}\colon f^{\ast}E\times f^{\ast}E\to f^{\ast}E

\displaystyle f^{\ast}\hat{p}_{E}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\prime}f^{\ast}E\to f^{\ast}E

uniquely defined as the lifts of the structure morphisms of the differential object
A
which commute with
\varphi_{f}
is the obvious way. Since
f^{\ast}\colon\Pi^{-1}(B)\to\Pi^{-1}(A)
is functorial, the equational axioms which make
f^{\ast}E\>\colon\!=(f^{\ast}E,f^{\ast}\zeta_{E},f^{\ast}\sigma_{E},f^{\ast}\hat{p}_{E})
into a differential object hold. Thus, we only need to prove the universal property of
f^{\ast}\hat{p}_{E}
. Consider the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\ast}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}f^{\ast}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\ast}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}_{f^{\ast}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\hat{p}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\varphi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{p}_{E}}

By the universal property of
\hat{p}_{E}
, there exists a unique morphism
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{E}\colon X\to\mathrm{T}^{\prime}E
making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\ast}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}f^{\ast}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\ast}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}_{f^{\ast}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\hat{p}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\varphi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{p}_{E}}

However, this diagram lifts the diagram in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Pi(X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi(\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\Pi(\alpha),\Pi(\beta)\rangle_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{p}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{p}_{B}}

Thus, by the universal property of
\hat{p}_{A}
and by the universal property of
\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\varphi_{f}
, there exists a unique
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{f^{\ast}E}\colon X\to\mathrm{T}^{\prime}f^{\ast}E
such that
\Pi(\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{f^{\ast}E})=\langle\Pi(\alpha),\Pi(\beta)\rangle_{A}\colon\Pi(X)\to\mathrm{T}A
, making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\ast}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}f^{\ast}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\ast}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{f^{\ast}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}_{f^{\ast}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\hat{p}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\varphi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{p}_{E}}

Thus,
f^{\ast}\hat{p}_{E}
is universal. We leave it to the reader to show that
\varphi_{f}
is Cartesian in
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
.
∎

Theorem 6.16
.

The
2
-category
\mathsf{FIB}
of (cloven) fibrations admits the construction of differential objects. In particular, the tangentad of differential objects of a (cloven) Cartesian tangent fibration
\Pi
is the Cartesian tangent fibration
\mathsf{DO}(\Pi)\colon\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Proof.

Consider a tangent fibration
\Pi_{\bullet}\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime}_{\bullet},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet})\to(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet})
and a morphism
(G,\beta;G^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\colon\Pi_{\bullet}\to\Pi
of tangent fibrations, together with a differential object structure in the Hom-tangent category
[\Pi_{\bullet},\Pi]
. Concretely, this consists of a list of natural transformations

\displaystyle\zeta_{A}

\displaystyle\colon\ast\to GA

\displaystyle\zeta^{\prime}_{E}

\displaystyle\colon\ast\to G^{\prime}E

\displaystyle\sigma_{A}

\displaystyle\colon GA\times GA\to GA

\displaystyle\sigma^{\prime}_{E}

\displaystyle\colon G^{\prime}E\times G^{\prime}E\to G^{\prime}E

\displaystyle\hat{p}_{A}

\displaystyle\colon\mathrm{T}GA\to GA

\displaystyle\hat{p}^{\prime}_{E}

\displaystyle\colon\mathrm{T}^{\prime}G^{\prime}E\to G^{\prime}E

for each
A\in(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet})
and
E\in(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet}^{\prime})
, such that
(GA,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})
and
(G^{\prime}E,\zeta_{E}^{\prime},\sigma_{E}^{\prime},\hat{p}_{E}^{\prime})
are differential objects in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
, respectively and satisfying the following equations:

\displaystyle\zeta_{\Pi_{\bullet}(E)}=\Pi(\zeta^{\prime}_{E})

\displaystyle\sigma_{\Pi_{\bullet}(E)}=\Pi(\sigma^{\prime}_{E})
